The zero inertia limit of Ericksen-Leslie’s model for liquid crystals
Ning Jiang, Yi-Long Luo

Abstract
. In this paper we study the zero inertia limit that is from the hyperbolic to parabolic Ericksen-Leslie’s liquid crystal flow. By introducing an initial layer and constructing an energy norm and energy dissipation functional depending on the solutions of the limiting system, we derive a global in time uniform energy bound to the remainder system under the small size of the initial data. Keywords. Zero inertia limit; Ericksen-Leslie model; Uniform energy bounds; Initial layer AMS subject classifications. 35L05; 35L30; 35L81; 37D50; 80A20



1.
Introduction and main theorem

The hydrodynamic theory of incompressible liquid crystals was established by Ericksen
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
and Leslie
[
11
,
12
]
in the 1960’s (see also Section 5.1 of
[
17
]
). In this paper, we study the following hyperbolic Ericksen-Leslie’s incompressible liquid crystal flow in
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{3}

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{t}\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{4}\Delta\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p^{\varepsilon}=-\mathrm{div}(\nabla\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\odot\nabla\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})+\mathrm{div}\bm{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}\,,\\[8.53581pt]
\mathrm{div}\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}=0\,,\\[8.53581pt]
\varepsilon\mathrm{D}^{2}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}=\Delta\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}+\bm{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}+\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{B}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})+\lambda_{2}\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\,,\\[8.53581pt]
|\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|=1\,,\end{array}\right.

where the Lagrangian multiplier
\bm{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}
(for the geometric constraint
|\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|=1
) is

(1.2)

\bm{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}=-\varepsilon|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|\nabla\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\lambda_{2}\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}:\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\otimes\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\,,

and the extra stress
\bm{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}
is

(1.3)

\displaystyle\bm{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}\equiv\bm{\sigma}(\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})=

\displaystyle\mu_{1}(\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}:\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\otimes\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\otimes\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}+\mu_{2}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{B}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})\otimes\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mu_{3}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\otimes(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{B}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})+\mu_{5}(\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})\otimes\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}+\mu_{6}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\otimes(\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})\,.

Here
\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the bulk velocity,
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
is the unit director fields of the liquid molecules,
p^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}
is the pressure. The notation
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
denotes the first order material derivative of
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
with respect to the velocity
\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}
by

(1.4)

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}=\partial_{t}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\,,

and
\mathrm{D}^{2}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
represents the second order material derivative,

(1.5)

\mathrm{D}^{2}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})\,.

In this paper we work on the periodic spatial domain
\mathbb{T}^{3}=\mathbb{R}^{3}/\mathbb{L}^{3}
, where
\mathbb{L}^{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is any
3
-dimensional lattice. The notations
\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}{}^{\top})
and
\mathrm{B}^{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}{}^{\top})
represent the rate of strain tensor, skew-symmetric part of the strain rate of by fluid velocity, respectively. More precisely, the entries of
\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{B}^{\varepsilon}
are given as

(1.6)

\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}_{i}+\partial_{i}\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}_{j})\,,\ \mathrm{B}^{\varepsilon}_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}_{i}-\partial_{i}\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}_{j})

for
1\leq i,j\leq 3
. One notices
\mathrm{B}^{\varepsilon}_{ij}=-\mathrm{B}^{\varepsilon}_{ji}
. The components of the vector
\mathrm{B}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
are
(\mathrm{B}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})_{i}=\mathrm{B}^{\varepsilon}_{ki}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}_{k}
and
(\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})_{i}=\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}_{ki}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}_{k}
, respectively. The entries of the matrix
\nabla\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\odot\nabla\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
are
(\nabla\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\odot\nabla\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})_{ij}=\partial_{i}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}_{k}\partial_{j}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}_{k}
and the symbol
a\otimes b
means
(a\otimes b)_{ij}=a_{i}b_{j}
for
1\leq i,j\leq 3
. For any two matrix
M,N\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
, we denote by
M:N=M_{ij}N_{ij}
. Furthermore, the symbol
\mathrm{div}M
means a vector field in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with the components
(\mathrm{div}M)_{i}=\partial_{j}M_{ij}
for
1\leq i\leq 3
. We emphasize that the Einstein summation convention is used throughout this paper.

The parameter
\varepsilon>0
is the inertia constant, which is usually small in the physical experiments.
\mu_{4}>0
is the viscosity of the flow. The material coefficients
\lambda_{1}\leq 0
and
\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
reflect the molecular shape and the slippery part between the fluid and the particles. The coefficients
\mu_{i}(i=1,2,3,5,6)
, which may depend on material and temperature, are usually called Leslie coefficients, and are related to certain local correlations in the fluid. Moreover, the previous coefficients have the relations
\mu_{4}>0
and

(1.7)

\lambda_{1}=\mu_{2}-\mu_{3}\,,\quad\lambda_{2}=\mu_{5}-\mu_{6}\,,\quad\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}=\mu_{6}-\mu_{5}\,.

The first two relations are necessary conditions in order to satisfy the equation of motion identically, while the third relation is called
Parodi’s relation
, which is derived from Onsager reciprocal relations expressing the equality of certain relations between flows and forces in thermodynamic systems out of equilibrium.

For the system (
1.1
) we take the initial data independent of
\varepsilon
, i.e.,

(1.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=\mathrm{u}^{in}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\,,\ \mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=\mathrm{d}^{in}(x)\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\,,(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})(0,x)=\tilde{\mathrm{d}}^{in}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

with the compatibilities

(1.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\mathrm{u}^{in}(x)=0\,,\ \mathrm{d}^{in}(x)\cdot\tilde{\mathrm{d}}^{in}(x)=0\,.

1.1.
Initial layer vs well-prepared initial data

Formally letting
\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathrm{u}_{0}
and
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathrm{d}_{0}
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
in the hyperbolic liquid crystal system (
1.1
) deduces to the parabolic liquid crystal model

(1.10)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{t}\mathrm{u}_{0}+\mathrm{u}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{u}_{0}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{4}\Delta\mathrm{u}_{0}+\nabla p_{0}=-\mathrm{div}(\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}\odot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0})+\mathrm{div}\bm{\sigma}_{0}\,,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{div}\mathrm{u}_{0}=0\,,\\[5.69054pt]
-\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}}\mathrm{d}_{0}+\mathrm{B}_{0}\mathrm{d}_{0})=\Delta\mathrm{d}_{0}+\bm{\gamma}_{0}\mathrm{d}_{0}+\lambda_{2}\mathrm{A}_{0}\mathrm{d}_{0}\,,\\[5.69054pt]
|\mathrm{d}_{0}|=1\,,\end{array}\right.

where the Lagrangian multiplier
\bm{\gamma}_{0}
is

(1.11)

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}_{0}=|\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}|^{2}-\lambda_{2}\mathrm{A}_{0}:\mathrm{d}_{0}\otimes\mathrm{d}_{0}\,,

and the extra stress
\bm{\sigma}_{0}
is

(1.12)

\displaystyle\bm{\sigma}_{0}\equiv

\displaystyle\bm{\sigma}(\mathrm{u}_{0},\mathrm{d}_{0})\,,

i.e. replacing
(\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})
by
(\mathrm{u}_{0},\mathrm{d}_{0})
in (
1.3
). Furthermore, the initial data of the limit system (
1.10
) will naturally be

(1.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}_{0}(0,x)=\mathrm{u}^{in}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\,,\ \mathrm{d}_{0}(0,x)=\mathrm{d}^{in}(x)\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\,.

The limit considered in this paper is a limit from a hyperbolic-type system for
\varepsilon>0
to a parabolic system for
\varepsilon=0
. One notices that
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
-equation in (
1.1
) is a system of wave equations with two initial conditions, while the
\mathrm{d}_{0}
-equation in (
1.10
) is a parabolic system with only a single initial condition. In general, the solution
(\mathrm{u}_{0},\mathrm{d}_{0})
to the limit system (
1.10
)-(
1.13
) does not satisfy the third initial condition in (
1.8
). To overcome this disparity, we will take two ways: 1) introduce an
initial layer
in times; 2) give a well-prepared initial condition.

Initial layer.
This disparity between the initial conditions of the hyperbolic type system (
1.1
) and of the parabolic type system (
1.10
) indicates that one should expect an
initial layer
in time, appearing in the limit process
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Specifically, this disparity, denoted by
\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)
, between the initial conditions (
1.8
) and (
1.13
) is defined as

(1.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}^{in}:=\tilde{\mathrm{d}}^{in}-\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}}\mathrm{d}_{0}|_{t=0}=\tilde{\mathrm{d}}^{in}+\mathrm{B}^{in}\mathrm{d}^{in}+\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{1}}(\Delta\mathrm{d}^{in}+\bm{\gamma}_{0}^{in}\mathrm{d}^{in}+\lambda_{2}\mathrm{A}^{in}\mathrm{d}^{in})\,.

Here
\mathrm{A}^{in}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\nabla\mathrm{u}^{in}+(\nabla\mathrm{u}^{in})^{\top})
,
\mathrm{B}^{in}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\nabla\mathrm{u}^{in}-(\nabla\mathrm{u}^{in})^{\top})
and
\bm{\gamma}_{0}^{in}=|\nabla\mathrm{d}^{in}|^{2}-\lambda_{2}\mathrm{A}^{in}:\mathrm{d}^{in}\otimes\mathrm{d}^{in}
.

We will justify rigorously this limit by employing the Hilbert expansion method in which the leading term is given by solutions to the limit system (
1.10
). In this approach, the solution to the limit system (
1.10
) is known beforehand. Then a special class of solutions to the original system (
1.1
) can be constructed around the limit system. A key of this approach is to construct a correct ansatz of the solutions to the original system. (
1.1
). Besides the limit system (
1.10
) and the remainder term
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
, an initial layer
\varepsilon^{\beta}\mathrm{d}_{I}(\tfrac{t}{\varepsilon^{\beta}},x)
to adsorb the disparity
\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)
should be included in the ansatz. More precisely, we take the following ansatz of the solution
(\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})
to the system (
1.1
)

(1.15)

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{u}_{0}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,,\ \mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{d}_{0}(t,x)+\varepsilon^{\beta}\mathrm{d}_{I}(\tfrac{t}{\varepsilon^{\beta}},x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)

for a fixed
\beta>0
to be determined, where
(\mathrm{u}_{0},\mathrm{d}_{0})
is the solution to (
1.10
) with initial data (
1.13
).

It is easy to derive by plugging the expansions (
1.15
) into the system (
1.1
) that the leading relation is

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{1-\beta}\partial^{2}_{\tau\tau}\mathrm{d}_{I}-\lambda_{1}\partial_{\tau}\mathrm{d}_{I}=\varepsilon^{\beta}\Delta\mathrm{d}_{I}\,,

where
\tau=\frac{t}{\varepsilon^{\beta}}
. Then we can design the initial layer satisfying the following linear damped wave system on
(\tau,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{3}
(called
initial layer system
):

(1.16)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial^{2}_{\tau\tau}\mathrm{d}_{I}+\tfrac{-\lambda_{1}}{\varepsilon^{1-\beta}}\partial_{\tau}\mathrm{d}_{I}=\varepsilon^{2\beta-1}\Delta\mathrm{d}_{I}\,,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{d}_{I}(\infty,x)=\lim\limits_{\tau\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{d}_{I}(\tau,x)=0\,,\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{\tau}\mathrm{d}_{I}(0,x)=\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)\,.\end{array}\right.

We emphasize that if
\beta>\tfrac{1}{2}
, the Laplacian term
\varepsilon^{2\beta-1}\Delta\mathrm{d}_{I}
is also a higher order term as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
., which means that it can be ignored. For instance, in Jiang-Luo-Tang-Zarnescu’s work
[
10
]
to justify this limit corresponding to the background velocity
\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}\equiv 0
, the value of
\beta
is taken as
\beta=1
, and ignored the term
\varepsilon\Delta\mathrm{d}_{I}
. For completeness and generality (for example, if we consider the case
\lambda_{1}=0
in a forthcoming separate paper, the term
\varepsilon^{2\beta-1}\Delta\mathrm{d}_{I}
must kept, and
\beta
should be taken as
1/2
), we keep this term in the initial layer structure here. Then, we can easily solve (
1.16
):

(1.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{I}(\tau,x)=2\varepsilon^{1-\beta}\Big(\lambda_{1}-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}+4\varepsilon\Delta}\Big)^{-1}\exp\Big(\tfrac{\lambda_{1}-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}+4\varepsilon\Delta}}{2\varepsilon^{1-\beta}}\tau\Big)\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)\,.

Here the operator
\Big(\lambda_{1}-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}+4\varepsilon\Delta}\Big)^{-1}\exp\Big(\tfrac{\lambda_{1}-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}+4\varepsilon\Delta}}{2\varepsilon^{1-\beta}}\tau\Big)
is understood in the sense of Fourier multiplier. As a consequence, the initial layer
\mathrm{d}_{I}
designed in (
1.17
) can be explicitly presented as

(1.18)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\beta}\mathrm{d}_{I}(\tfrac{t}{\varepsilon^{\beta}},x)=\varepsilon\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x):=2\varepsilon\Big(\lambda_{1}-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}+4\varepsilon\Delta}\Big)^{-1}\exp\Big(\tfrac{\lambda_{1}-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}+4\varepsilon\Delta}}{2\varepsilon}t\Big)\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)\,.

One observes that
\varepsilon^{\beta}\mathrm{d}_{I}(\tfrac{t}{\varepsilon^{\beta}},x)
is independent of
\beta>0
. In other words, this initial layer is an intrinsic structure resulted from the disparity
\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)
of the initial conditions between the original system (
1.1
) and the limit equations (
1.10
). Since the real part
\Re e\big(\lambda_{1}-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}-4\varepsilon|k|^{2}}\big)<0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, both the initial layer
\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
and its time derivative
\partial_{t}\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
exponentially decay to zero as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
for every
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{3}
. This means that the disparity
\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)
just affects the whole evolution process in a very short beginning time.

We now write the ansatz (
1.15
) as the form

(1.19)

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{u}_{0}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,,\ \mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{d}_{0}(t,x)+\varepsilon\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,.

Then, plugging the ansatz (
1.19
) into the original system (
1.1
) implies that the remainder
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
satisfies the following system

(1.20)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{t}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{4}\Delta\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p_{R}^{\varepsilon}=\mu_{1}\mathrm{div}\big[(\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon}:\mathrm{d}_{0}\otimes\mathrm{d}_{0})\mathrm{d}_{0}\otimes\mathrm{d}_{0}\big]\\
\qquad\qquad+\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{u}}+\mathrm{div}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}}+\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{u}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{u}})+\varepsilon\mathrm{div}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{u}}(\mathrm{D}_{I})\,,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{div}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}=0\,,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{D}^{2}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\tfrac{-\lambda_{1}}{\varepsilon}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\Delta\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon})\\
=\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\mathrm{d}}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}+\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{d}}+\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{d}}(\mathrm{D}_{I})\end{array}\right.

with the constraint

(1.21)

\displaystyle 2\mathrm{d}_{0}\cdot(\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon})+\sqrt{\varepsilon}|\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}=0\,,

where the tensor
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}}
is

(1.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}}=

\displaystyle\mu_{2}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0})\otimes\mathrm{d}_{0}+\mu_{3}\mathrm{d}_{0}\otimes(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0})

\displaystyle+\mu_{5}(\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0})\otimes\mathrm{d}_{0}+\mu_{6}\mathrm{d}_{0}\otimes(\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0})\,,

the vector field
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}}
is

(1.23)

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}}=\lambda_{1}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0}+\lambda_{2}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0}\,,

and the tedious terms
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{u}}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{u}}
,
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\mathrm{d}}
,
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}
,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{d}}
,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{u}}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{u}}(\mathrm{D}_{I})
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{d}}(\mathrm{D}_{I})
are defined as the forms (
A.2
), (
A.3
), (
A.4
), (
A.5
), (
A.6
), (
A.7
), (
A.12
) and (
A.21
) in Appendix
A
respectively.

Next we consider the initial conditions of the remainder system (
1.20
). Since our goal is to seek a solution to (
1.1
) with the form (
1.19
), the initial data of
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
should subject to

(1.24)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)-\mathrm{u}_{0}(0,x)\,,\\
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)-\mathrm{d}_{0}(0,x)-\varepsilon\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)\,,\\
\sqrt{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})(0,x)=(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})(0,x)-(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}}\mathrm{d}_{0})(0,x)-\sqrt{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0})(0,x)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\varepsilon\partial_{t}\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)-\varepsilon(\mathrm{u}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon})(0,x)-\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{3}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon})(0,x)\,.\end{array}\right.

Recalling that the initial conditions of the original system (
1.1
) and the limit system (
1.10
) satisfy

(1.25)

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=\mathrm{u}_{0}(0,x)=\mathrm{u}^{in}(x)\,,\ \mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=\mathrm{d}_{0}(0,x)=\mathrm{d}^{in}(x)\,,(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})(0,x)=\tilde{\mathrm{d}}^{in}(x)

and the initial data of the initial layer
\mathrm{d}_{I}
in (
1.16
) is imposed on

(1.26)

\displaystyle\varepsilon\partial_{t}\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=\partial_{\tau}\mathrm{d}_{I}(0,x)=\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)\,,

where
\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)
is the disparity defined in (
1.14
), we derive from the initial relations (
1.24
) that the initial data of the remainder system (
1.20
) should be

(1.27)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=0\,,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}^{in}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,,\\[5.69054pt]
(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})(0,x)=-\sqrt{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{u}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon})(0,x)=-\sqrt{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{u}^{in}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}^{in}_{\varepsilon})(x)\,,\end{array}\right.

where the vector field
\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}^{in}_{\varepsilon}(x)
is defined as

(1.28)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}^{in}_{\varepsilon}(x)=2\Big(\lambda_{1}-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}+4\varepsilon\Delta}\Big)^{-1}\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)\,.

We emphasize that for any fixed
s\geq 0
, the norm bound

(1.29)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}^{in}_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\leq\tfrac{4}{\lambda_{1}^{2}}\|\mathrm{D}^{in}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}

holds uniformly in
\varepsilon>0
, since the uniform lower bound
\big|\lambda_{1}-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}-4\varepsilon|k|^{2}}\big|\geq-\lambda_{1}>0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
reduces to

(1.30)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}^{in}_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}(1+|k|^{2})^{s}\tfrac{4}{\big|\lambda_{1}-\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}-4\varepsilon|k|^{2}}\big|^{2}}\big|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)e^{-ix\cdot k}\mathrm{d}x\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tfrac{4}{\lambda_{1}^{2}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}(1+|k|^{2})^{s}\big|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)e^{-ix\cdot k}\mathrm{d}x\big|^{2}=\tfrac{4}{\lambda_{1}^{2}}\|\mathrm{D}^{in}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\,.

Here the
H^{s}
-norm will be defined in the later.

Well-prepared initial data.
As mentioned before, one of our goal in this paper is to deal with the disparity
\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)
given in (
1.14
) resulted from the initial conditions (
1.8
). Besides introducing a so-called initial layer in time to overcome this disparity, we can also impose the original system (
1.1
) on the so-called
well-prepared initial data
. To be more precise, we can skilfully select the initial values (
1.8
) of the system (
1.1
) such that the disparity
\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)
vanishes, hence

(1.31)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathrm{d}}^{in}(x)=(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}}\mathrm{d}_{0})(0,x)=-(\mathrm{B}^{in}\mathrm{d}^{in})(x)-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{1}}(\Delta\mathrm{d}^{in}+\bm{\gamma}_{0}^{in}\mathrm{d}^{in}+\lambda_{2}\mathrm{A}^{in}\mathrm{d}^{in})(x)\,.

Actually, if the disparity
\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)=0
, the initial layer
\varepsilon\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
defined in (
1.18
) will automatically be zero. Consequently, we shall take ansatz that the system (
1.1
) imposed on the well-prepared initial data (
1.8
)-(
1.31
) has a solution
(\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})
with the form

(1.32)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{u}_{0}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{d}_{0}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,.\end{array}\right.

By substituting the ansatz (
1.32
) into the original hyperbolic type system (
1.1
), one easily derives that the remainder
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
subjects to the system with the similar structure of (
1.20
), just removing the terms
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon})
,
\varepsilon\mathrm{div}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{u}}(\mathrm{D}_{I})
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{d}}(\mathrm{D}_{I})
appearing in the system (
1.20
). More precisely, the remainder
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
in (
1.32
) satisfies

(1.33)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{t}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{4}\Delta\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p_{R}^{\varepsilon}=\mu_{1}\mathrm{div}\big[(\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon}:\mathrm{d}_{0}\otimes\mathrm{d}_{0})\mathrm{d}_{0}\otimes\mathrm{d}_{0}\big]\\
\qquad\qquad+\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{u}}+\mathrm{div}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}}+\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{u}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{u}})\,,\\[2.84526pt]
\mathrm{div}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}=0\,,\\[2.84526pt]
\mathrm{D}^{2}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\tfrac{-\lambda_{1}}{\varepsilon}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\Delta\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0})\\
=\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\mathrm{d}}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}+\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{d}}\end{array}\right.

with the constraint

(1.34)

\displaystyle 2\mathrm{d}_{0}\cdot\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}|\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}=0\,,

where the tensor term
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}}
and the vector term
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}}
are defined in (
1.22
) and (
1.23
) respectively, and the accurate expressions of the tedious terms
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{u}}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{u}}
,
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\mathrm{d}}
,
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}
,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{d}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{u}}
are all given in Appendix
A
. Furthermore, based on the initial relations (
1.24
), we know that the initial conditions of the remainder system (
1.33
) should be imposed on

(1.35)

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=0\,,\ \mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=0\,,\ (\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})(0,x)=0\,.

1.2.
Main results.

To state our main results, we collect here the notations we will use throughout this paper. We denote by
A\thicksim B
if there are two constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
, independent of
\varepsilon>0
, such that
C_{1}A\leq B\leq C_{2}A
. For convenience, we also denote by

L^{p}=L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{3})

for all
p\in[1,\infty]
, which endows with the norm
\|f\|_{L^{p}}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}|f(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
for
p\in[1,\infty)
and
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}=\underset{x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}}{\textrm{ess sup}}\,|f(x)|
. For
p=2
, we use the notation
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle
to represent the inner product on the Hilbert space
L^{2}
.

For any multi-index
m=(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})
in
\mathbb{N}^{3}
, we denote the
m^{th}
partial derivative by

\partial^{m}=\partial^{m_{1}}_{x_{1}}\partial^{m_{2}}_{x_{2}}\partial^{m_{3}}_{x_{3}}\,.

If each component of
m\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
is not greater than that of
\tilde{m}
’s, we denote by
m\leq\tilde{m}
. The symbol
m<\tilde{m}
means
m\leq\tilde{m}
and
|m|<|\tilde{m}|
, where
|m|=m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}
. We define the Sobolev space
H^{N}=H^{N}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
by the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{N}}=\bigg(\sum_{|m|\leq N}\|\partial^{m}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty\,,

or the equivalent norm

(1.36)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{N}}=\Big(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}(1+|k|^{2})^{s}|\widehat{f}(k)|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}<+\infty\,,

where the symbol
\widehat{f}(k)
is the Fourier transform of
f(x)
on
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
, hence,

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(k)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}f(x)e^{ix\cdot k}\mathrm{d}x

fro all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
. For any integer
N\geq 2
, we define a number
S_{\!{}_{N}}\in\mathbb{N}
as

(1.37)

\displaystyle S_{\!{}_{N}}=\min\{k\in\mathbb{N};2k\geq N+2\}\,.

Actually, if
N
is even,
S_{\!{}_{N}}=\tfrac{1}{2}N+1
and if
N
is odd,
S_{\!{}_{N}}=\tfrac{1}{2}(N+3)
.

Now we state our main theorem:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
N\geq 2
be an integer and
(\mathrm{u}^{in}(x),\mathrm{d}^{in}(x),\tilde{\mathrm{d}}^{in}(x))\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfy the compatibility conditions (
1.9
) and
\mathrm{u}^{in}\,,\tilde{\mathrm{d}}^{in}\,,\nabla\mathrm{d}^{in}\in H^{2S_{\!{}_{N}}}
. If the Leslie’s coefficients satisfy

(1.38)

\displaystyle\mu_{4}>0\,,\ \lambda_{1}<0\,,\ \mu_{1}\geq 0\,,\ \mu_{5}+\mu_{6}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{\lambda_{1}}\geq 0

and there exist small
\varepsilon_{0},\xi_{0}\in(0,1]
, depending on the Leslie’s coefficients and
N
, such that

(1.39)

\displaystyle E^{in}\overset{\Delta}{=}\|\mathrm{u}^{in}\|^{2}_{H^{2S_{\!{}_{N}}}}+\|\tilde{\mathrm{d}}^{in}\|^{2}_{H^{2S_{\!{}_{N}}}}+\|\nabla\mathrm{d}^{in}\|^{2}_{H^{2S_{\!{}_{N}}}}\leq\xi_{0}

for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, then the system (
1.1
) with the initial conditions (
1.8
) admits a unique solution
(\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})
satisfying

(1.40)

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon},\nabla\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{N})\,,\ \nabla\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{N})\,.

Moreover, the solution
(\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})
is of the form

(1.41)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{u}_{0}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{d}_{0}(t,x)+\varepsilon\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,,\end{array}\right.

where
(\mathrm{u}_{0},\mathrm{d}_{0})
is the solution to the incompressible parabolic Ericksen-Leslie’s liquid crystal model (
1.10
) with the initial data (
1.13
), the initial layer
\varepsilon\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
is defined in (
1.18
), and
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
obeys the remainder system (
1.20
) with the initial condition (
1.27
). Furthermore,
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
satisfies the uniform energy bound

(1.42)

\displaystyle\Big(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\|\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}+\|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\|\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N+1}}\Big)(t)+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\leq C\xi_{0}

for all
t\geq 0
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
and for some constant
C>0
, independent of
\varepsilon
and
t
.

Remark 1.1
.

The small number
\xi_{0}
is firstly smaller than
\beta_{S_{\!{}_{N}},0}
mentioned in Proposition
3.1
proved by Wang-Zhang-Zhang in
[
21
]
, so that the limit system (
1.10
)-(
1.13
) admits a unique global in time classical solution
(\mathrm{u}_{0},\mathrm{d}_{0})
under the constraint of small size
\xi_{0}
to the initial data. Thus, the vectors
\mathrm{u}_{0}
and
\mathrm{d}_{0}
appeared in the remainder system (
1.20
) can be regarded as the known coefficients.

Remark 1.2
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, if the well-prepared initial data is further assumed, i.e., (
1.31
) holds, then the hyperbolic Ericksen-Leslie’s liquid crystal system (
1.1
)-(
1.8
) has a unique global classical solution
(\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})
with the form

(1.43)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{u}_{0}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{d}_{0}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,,\end{array}\right.

and the other conclusions are the same as stated in Theorem
1.1
.

Remark 1.3
.

The uniform bound (
1.42
) implies that

(1.44)

\|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{N}})+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\|\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{N+1})}\leq C\xi_{0}\,,

which shows us the convergence rate
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
on the orientation
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
we obtain is optimal. This optimality can be also seen in Remark 1.1 of Jiang-Luo-Tang-Zarnescu’s work
[
10
]
. We also derive from the uniform bound (
1.42
) that

(1.45)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\|\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{N})}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\|\nabla\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{N})}\leq C\xi_{0}\,.

Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{u}}_{R}^{\varepsilon}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
. Then the expansion of
\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}
can be rewritten as
\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{u}_{0}(t,x)+\varepsilon\widetilde{\mathrm{u}}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
and the uniform bound
\|\widetilde{\mathrm{u}}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{N})}+\|\nabla\widetilde{\mathrm{u}}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{N})}\leq C\xi_{0}
holds, which means that the convergence rate of the velocity
\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}
is
\varepsilon
.

1.3.
Ideals and novelties

Generally speaking, there are three aspects of the disparity between the original system and the limit equations on the limit problem. First, the form of the limit equations is obviously different from that of the original system. Second, the initial conditions of original system and limit equations are not the same type, which will result to the initial layers. Finally, the boundary conditions will also lead to the disparity, which can be covered by the boundary layers.

In the current paper, we study the limit problem on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
in the regime of classical solutions, which does not involve the boundary conditions. It is a very difficult problem to derive the energy bounds of
(\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})
uniform in small
\varepsilon>0
to the original hyperbolic Ericksen-Leslie’s liquid crystal model (
1.1
). So, we take the Hilbert expansion method to rigorously justify the limit from hyperbolic Ericksen-Leslie’s liquid crystal model to the parabolic case, in which the remainder term
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
is utilized to deal with the difference between the forms of two systems. Because the
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
-equation in (
1.1
) is a wave type equation and the corresponding limit
\mathrm{d}_{0}
-equation in (
1.10
) is a parabolic type equation, the
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
-equation of original system need impose on two initial conditions but the
\mathrm{d}_{0}
-equation of limit system only need impose on one initial data. One of the methods to overcome this disparity is to introduce an initial layer
\varepsilon\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
(defined in (
1.18
)), so that the disparity
\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)
is absorbed, for details see the analysis before. Thus we give the formal expansion (
1.19
), namely

\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{u}_{0}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,,\ \mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{d}_{0}(t,x)+\varepsilon\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,.

Another method to overcome the disparity of the initial conditions is to give the well-prepared initial data such that
\mathrm{D}^{in}(x)=0
, which immediately implies that the initial layer
\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
vanishes. In this case, the expansion form will be (
1.32
), hence

\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{u}_{0}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,,\ \mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{d}_{0}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,.

The main goal of this work is to derive the global energy bound of the remainder
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
uniform in small
\varepsilon>0
under the small size of the initial data
(\mathrm{u}^{in},\mathrm{d}^{in},\tilde{\mathrm{d}}^{in})
. Since the initial layer
\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
and its time derivative
\partial_{t}\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
are infinitely small as
\varepsilon\ll 1
, we can merely consider the remainder
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
in the expansion (
1.32
) with respect to the case of well-prepared initial data, which satisfying the system (
1.33
). The major structure of (
1.33
) reads

(1.46)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{t}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{4}\Delta\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\mathrm{div}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}}=\textrm{some other terms}\,,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{div}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}=0\,,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}^{2}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{\lambda_{1}}{\varepsilon}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\Delta\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0})-\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}}=\textrm{some other terms}\,,\end{array}\right.

where
\lambda_{1}<0
. The
key point
in this work is how to control the term
\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0})
in the energy estimates to the remainder system (
1.33
). We will design a energy functional, which sensitively depends on the limit vector field
(\mathrm{u}_{0},\mathrm{d}_{0})
, to deal with this term. More precisely, we multiply by
\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
in the first and third equations of (
1.46
) respectively and integrate by parts over
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
. Then, combining the cancellation (
2.1
) of the case
m=0
in Lemma
2.1
, hence

\displaystyle-\left\langle\mathrm{div}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}},\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}},\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle=

\displaystyle-\lambda_{1}\|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+(\mu_{5}+\mu_{6}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{\lambda_{1}})\|\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,,

we obtain the main part of the
L^{2}
-energy equality

(1.47)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big(\underset{The\ energy}{\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\|\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\|\nabla\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}}}\big)+\left\langle\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}),\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\underset{The\ energy\ dissipative\ rate}{\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\mu_{4}\|\nabla\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\tfrac{\lambda_{1}}{\varepsilon}\|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(\mu_{5}+\mu_{6}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{\lambda_{1}})\|\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}}}

\displaystyle=\cdots\cdots

Under the coefficient conditions (
1.38
), the energy dissipative rate is positive. If we regard the term
\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0})
as a source term, the quantity
\left\langle\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}),\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle
should be dominated by the energy and the energy dissipative rate defined in the above equality. However, it is impossible, since the regularity of
\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0})
is equivalent to
\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{4}\Delta\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
by using the first
\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
-equation of (
1.46
) and the highest order regularity of the energy dissipative rate is
\nabla\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
. In order to overcome this difficulty, we try to design this quantity as a part of the energy. More precisely, we take the following
important
deformation

(1.48)

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}),\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\langle\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0},\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0},\partial_{t}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\langle\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0},\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0},\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}^{2}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\left\langle\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0},(\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big(\|\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+2\left\langle\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0},\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\underset{P_{1}}{\underbrace{-\left\langle(\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}),\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\langle\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}}\right\rangle}}

\displaystyle\underset{P_{1}}{\underbrace{-\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\langle\nabla(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}),\nabla\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle+\tfrac{\lambda_{1}}{\varepsilon}\left\langle\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0},\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle}}

\displaystyle+\cdots\cdots

where the last equality is derived from the
\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
-equation of (
1.46
). Then, we obtain the relation

(1.49)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big(\underset{The\ new\ energy}{\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\|\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\|\nabla\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+2\left\langle\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0},\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle}}\Big)

\displaystyle+\underset{The\ energy\ dissipative\ rate}{\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\mu_{4}\|\nabla\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\tfrac{\lambda_{1}}{\varepsilon}\|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(\mu_{5}+\mu_{6}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{\lambda_{1}})\|\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}}}

\displaystyle=P_{1}+P_{2}+\cdots\cdots

where the quantities
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
can be controlled by the energy and energy dissipative rate. Although the new energy is not positive for all
\varepsilon>0
, it will be a definitely positive sign with sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
under the fixed coefficient
\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}
. Consequently, we have designed a complicated energy functional, which sensitively depends on the solutions to the limit system, to deal with the trouble quantity
\left\langle\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}),\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle
.

The advantage of the expansion (
1.32
) of the solutions
(\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})
to the system (
1.1
) is such that the remainder equations (
1.33
) of
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
have weaker nonlinearities than the original system (
1.1
), despite the system (
1.33
) is still nonlinear and singular (with singular terms of the type
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
). To be more precise, after utilizing the expansion (
1.32
), the nonlinearity and singularity are separated. For instance, the term
(-\varepsilon|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|\nabla\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\lambda_{2}\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}:\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\otimes\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
in the original system (
1.1
) is replaced by either linear terms ( the unknown
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}
are superseded by the known
\mathrm{u}_{0}
and
\mathrm{d}_{0}
) or a nonlinear term with the same form but with some higher order power of
\varepsilon
in front. So, it will be easier to get the energy bound, uniform in small
\varepsilon>0
, of the remainder system (
1.33
)-(
1.35
).

1.4.
Historical remarks

In this subsection, we review some history of the mathematical analytic works on the liquid crystals, in particular Ericksen-Leslie’s system. The static analogue of the parabolic Ericksen-Leslie’s system (
1.10
) is the so-called Oseen-Frank model, whose mathematical study was initialed from Hardt-Kinderlehrer-Lin
[
5
]
. Since then there have been many works in this direction. In particular, the existence and regularity or partial regularity of the approximation (usually Ginzburg-Landau approximation as in
[
14
]
) dynamical Ericksen-Leslie’s system was started by the work of Lin and Liu in
[
14
]
,
[
15
]
and
[
16
]
. For the simplest system preserving the basic energy law which can be obtained by neglecting the Leslie stress and by specifying some elastic constants, in 2-D case, the existence of global weak solutions with at most a finite number of singular times were proved by Lin-Lin-Wang
[
13
]
. Recently, Lin and Wang proved global existence of weak solution for 3-D case with the initial director field
\mathrm{d}^{in}(x)
lying in the hemisphere in
[
19
]
. For the more general parabolic Ericksen-Leslie’s system, local well-posedness is proved by Wang-Zhang-Zhang in
[
21
]
, and in
[
7
]
existence of global solutions and regularity in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
was established by Huang-Lin-Wang. For more complete review of the works for the parabolic Ericksen-Leslie’s system, please see the reference listed above.

For the hyperbolic Ericksen-Leslie’s system (
1.1
), much less is known. For the most simplified model, i.e. taking the bulk velocity field
\mathrm{u}=0
, neglecting the Leslie’s coefficients, and the spatial dimension is
1
, the system (
1.1
) can be reduced to a so-called nonlinear variational wave equation which is already highly nontrivial. Zhang and Zheng studied systematically the dissipative and energy conservative solutions in series work starting from late 90’s (
[
23
,
24
,
22
]
).

Recently, there started some works on the original hyperbolic Ericksen-Leslie’s system (
1.1
) for multi-dimentional case. the authors of the current paper studied in
[
8
]
the well-posedness in the context of classical solutions of (
1.1
). More precisely, in
[
8
]
under some natural constraints on the Leslie coefficients which ensure the basic energy law is dissipative, it was proved the local-in-time existence and uniqueness of the classical solution to the system (
1.1
) with finite initial energy. Furthermore, with an additional assumption on the coefficients which provides a damping effect, i.e.
\lambda_{1}<0
, and the smallness of the initial energy, the unique global classical solution was established. Here we remark that the assumption
\lambda_{1}<0
plays a crucial role in the global-in-time well-posedness. Later, Cai-Wang
[
1
]
made progress for the simplied Ericksen-Leslie system, namely, the case with
\mu_{i}=0,i=1\,,\cdots\,,6
,
i\neq 4
in (
1.3
). They proved the global regularity of (
1.1
) near the constant equilibrium by employing the vector field method. More recently, in
[
6
]
, the authors of the current papers with Huang and Zhao, considered the more general case: still
\mu_{2}=\mu_{3}=0
, but
0\neq\mu_{5}=\mu_{6}>-\mu_{4}
, and
0\neq\mu_{1}>-2(\mu_{4}+\mu_{5})
, and proved results similar to
[
1
]
.

Regarding to the inertia limit, i.e.
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, for a given bulk velocity and well-prepared initial data, together with Tang, we justified this limit in
[
9
]
. For the case without the bulk velocity and general initial data, by constructing an initial layer, we with Tang and Zarnescu, justified this limit in
[
10
]
. In this sense, the current paper, proved this inertia limit for the much more general case with bulk velocity field, under the assumption
\lambda_{1}<0
. The case
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=0
will be analytically more subtle, for which the limiting system is the harmonic map to
\mathbf{S}^{2}
, and furthermore, the initial layer will be a wave equation which preserve the energy. This work is under preparation, together with Huang and Zhao.

The organization of this paper is as follows: in the next section, we first derive the canceled relations between
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}}
contained in the remainder system of
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
, which will play an essential role in estimating the energy of the remainder
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
. Then we shown that the constraints (
1.21
) and (
1.34
) will hold at any time provided they initially hold, see Lemma
2.2
. In Section
3
, we estimate the uniform energy bound on small
\varepsilon>0
of the remainder system of
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
. Then, based on the uniform energy estimates in the previous section, Theorem
1.1
of the current paper is proved in Section
4
. Finally, we accurately present the all tedious term of the remainder system (
1.20
) (also (
1.33
)) in Appendix
A
.

2.
Some basic cancellations and constraints

In this section, we will first derive some basic cancellations on the remainder equations (
1.20
) (or (
1.33
)), which will play an essential role in deriving the global in time energy estimates uniformly in
\varepsilon>0
to the remainder system (
1.20
) (or (
1.33
)) with small initial data. We then prove that the constraints (
1.21
) or (
1.34
), which come from the geometric constraint
|\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|=1
in the original system (
1.1
), will hold for all time
t\geq 0
provided they initially hold.

First, we will give the following lemma, which displays the cancellations between the terms
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Under the relations (
1.7
), for all multi-indexes
m\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
, one has

(2.1)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{div}\partial^{m}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}},\partial^{m}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle+\left\langle\partial^{m}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}},\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\left\|\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\lambda_{1}\left\|\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle-(\mu_{5}+\mu_{6}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{\lambda_{1}})\left\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\mathcal{G}_{m}\,,

where the quantity
\mathcal{G}_{m}
is defined as follows:
if
m=0
,

(2.2)

\mathcal{G}_{m}=0\,,

and if
m\neq 0
,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{m}=

\displaystyle\sum_{m^{\prime}<m}C_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\Big[\left\langle\lambda_{1}(\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}_{0}+\lambda_{2}(\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}_{0},\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left\langle\mu_{2}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\mathrm{d}_{0,k})\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{B}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon}+\mu_{3}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\mathrm{d}_{0,k})\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{B}_{R,kj}^{\varepsilon},\partial^{m}\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}_{R,i}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left\langle\mu_{5}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\mathrm{d}_{0,k})\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{A}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon}+\mu_{6}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\mathrm{d}_{0,k})\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{A}_{R,kj}^{\varepsilon},\partial^{m}\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}_{R,i}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left\langle\mu_{2}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\partial^{m^{\prime}}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})_{i}+\mu_{3}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\partial^{m^{\prime}}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})_{j},\partial^{m}\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}_{R,i}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle\Big]\,.

Proof of Lemma
2.1
.

Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}}
in (
1.22
), one deduces

(2.4)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{div}\partial^{m}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}},\partial^{m}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle=

\displaystyle\underset{U_{1}}{\underbrace{\left\langle\partial_{j}\partial^{m}(\mu_{2}\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\mathrm{B}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0,k}+\mu_{3}\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\mathrm{B}_{R,kj}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0,k}),\partial^{m}\mathrm{u}_{R,i}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle}}

\displaystyle+\underset{U_{2}}{\underbrace{\left\langle\partial_{j}\partial^{m}(\mu_{5}\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\mathrm{d}_{0,k}\mathrm{A}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon}+\mu_{6}\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\mathrm{d}_{0,k}\mathrm{A}_{R,kj}^{\varepsilon}),\partial^{m}\mathrm{u}_{R,i}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle}}

\displaystyle+\underset{U_{3}}{\underbrace{\left\langle\partial_{j}\partial^{m}\big[\mu_{2}\mathrm{d}_{0,j}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})_{i}+\mu_{3}\mathrm{d}_{0,i}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})_{j}\big],\partial^{m}\mathrm{u}_{R,i}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle}}\,.

Then we will directly calculate the terms
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
and
U_{3}
for the case
m\neq 0
. The case
m=0
can be similarly justified. For the term
U_{1}
, we derive from the integration by parts over
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
that

(2.5)

\displaystyle U_{1}=\ \underset{U_{11}}{\underbrace{-\left\langle\mu_{2}\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0,k}+\mu_{3}\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R,kj}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{0,k},\partial^{m}\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}_{R,i}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle}}

\displaystyle\underset{U_{12}}{\underbrace{-\sum_{m^{\prime}<m}C_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\left\langle\mu_{2}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\mathrm{d}_{0,k})\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{B}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon}+\mu_{3}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\mathrm{d}_{0,k})\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{B}_{R,kj}^{\varepsilon},\partial^{m}\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}_{R,i}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle}}\,.

Since
\lambda_{1}=\mu_{2}-\mu_{3}
,
\lambda_{2}=\mu_{5}-\mu_{6}
and
\mu_{6}-\mu_{5}=\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}
, we have

(2.6)

\displaystyle U_{11}=

\displaystyle-\big\langle(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})\mathrm{d}_{0,j}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0,k}

\displaystyle+\mu_{3}(\mathrm{d}_{0,j}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0,k}+\mathrm{d}_{0,i}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R,kj}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0,k}),\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R,ij}^{\varepsilon}+\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R,ij}^{\varepsilon}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\lambda_{1}\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{0,j}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0,k},\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R,ij}^{\varepsilon}+\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R,ij}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle-\mu_{3}\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{0,j}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0,k}+\mathrm{d}_{0,i}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R,kj}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0,k},\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R,ij}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-(\lambda_{1}+2\mu_{3})\left\langle(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0,k},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R,ij}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\lambda_{2}\left\langle(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\rangle\,,

where we make use of the relation
\mathrm{B}_{R,ij}^{\varepsilon}=-\mathrm{B}_{R,ji}^{\varepsilon}
.

For the term
U_{2}
, integrating by parts over
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
implies

(2.7)

\displaystyle U_{2}=\underset{U_{21}}{\underbrace{-\left\langle\mu_{5}\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\mathrm{d}_{0,k}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon})+\mu_{6}\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\mathrm{d}_{0,k}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R,kj}^{\varepsilon}),\partial^{m}\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}_{R,i}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle}}

\displaystyle\underset{U_{22}}{\underbrace{-\sum_{m^{\prime}<m}C_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\left\langle\mu_{5}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\mathrm{d}_{0,k})\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{A}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon}+\mu_{6}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\mathrm{d}_{0,k})\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{A}_{R,kj}^{\varepsilon},\partial^{m}\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}_{R,i}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle}}\,.

By the similar calculations on
U_{11}
, we compute the term
U_{21}

(2.8)

\displaystyle U_{21}=

\displaystyle-\big\langle(\mu_{5}-\mu_{6})\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\mathrm{d}_{0,k}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle+\mu_{6}(\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\mathrm{d}_{0,k}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R,ki}^{\varepsilon})+\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\mathrm{d}_{0,k}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R,kj}^{\varepsilon})),\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{ij}^{\varepsilon}+\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{ij}^{\varepsilon}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\lambda_{2}\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\lambda_{2}\left\langle(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\rangle-2\mu_{6}\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\mu_{5}+\mu_{6})\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\lambda_{2}\left\langle(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\rangle\,.

For the term
U_{3}
, we deduce from integrating by parts over
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
that

(2.9)

\displaystyle U_{3}=\underset{U_{31}}{\underbrace{-\left\langle\mu_{2}\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\partial^{m}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})_{i}+\mu_{3}\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\partial^{m}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})_{j},\partial^{m}\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}_{R,i}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle}}

\displaystyle\underset{U_{32}}{\underbrace{-\sum_{m^{\prime}<m}C_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\left\langle\mu_{2}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\partial^{m^{\prime}}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})_{i}+\mu_{3}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\partial^{m^{\prime}}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})_{j},\partial^{m}\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}_{R,i}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle}}\,.

It is easily yielded that by the analogous arguments in computations of
U_{11}
or
U_{21}

(2.10)

\displaystyle U_{31}=

\displaystyle-\big\langle(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\partial^{m}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})_{i}

\displaystyle+\mu_{3}(\mathrm{d}_{0,j}\partial^{m}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})_{i}+\mathrm{d}_{0,i}\partial^{m}(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})_{j}),\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R,ij}^{\varepsilon}+\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R,ij}^{\varepsilon}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\lambda_{1}\left\langle\partial^{m}D_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\rangle-2\mu_{3}\left\langle\partial^{m}D_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\lambda_{1}\left\langle\partial^{m}D_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\left\langle\partial^{m}D_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\rangle+\lambda_{2}\left\langle\partial^{m}D_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\rangle\,.

We thereby obtain

(2.11)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{div}\partial^{m}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}},\partial^{m}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\lambda_{2}\left\langle\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+2(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\rangle

\displaystyle-(\mu_{5}+\mu_{6})\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\lambda_{1}\left\langle\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+U_{12}+U_{22}+U_{32}\,.

We finally compute the quantity
\left\langle\partial^{m}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}},\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle
. Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}}
in (
1.23
), we deduce

(2.12)

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial^{m}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}},\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\left\langle(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0},\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle+\lambda_{2}\left\langle(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0},\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\underset{U_{4}}{\underbrace{\sum_{m^{\prime}<m}C_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\left\langle\lambda_{1}(\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}_{0}+\lambda_{2}(\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}_{0},\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle}}\,.

Adding the equality (
2.11
) to (
2.12
) tells us

(2.13)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{div}\partial^{m}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}},\partial^{m}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle+\left\langle\partial^{m}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}},\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+2\lambda_{2}\left\langle\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\rangle

\displaystyle-(\mu_{5}+\mu_{6})\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+2\lambda_{1}\left\langle\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon},(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+U_{12}+U_{22}+U_{32}+U_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\left\|\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\lambda_{1}\left\|\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle-(\mu_{5}+\mu_{6}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{\lambda_{1}})\left\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}++U_{12}+U_{22}+U_{32}+U_{4}\,,

and then the proof of Lemma
2.1
is completed.
∎

Then, inspired by Lemma 4.1 of
[
8
]
, the work of the same two authors of this paper, we derive the following lemma to show how the constraints (
1.21
) or (
1.34
) hold under the corresponding initial constraints.

Lemma 2.2
.

Assume
(\mathrm{u}_{0},\mathrm{d}_{0})
and
(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})
are two sufficiently smooth solutions to the limit system (
1.10
)-(
1.13
) and the remainder system (
1.20
)-(
1.27
), respectively. Let
\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
is the initial layer defined in (
1.18
). If the initial constraint

(2.14)

\displaystyle 2(\mathrm{d}_{0}\cdot(\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}))(0,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}|\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}(0,x)=0

holds, then the constraint (
1.21
), i.e.,

(2.15)

\displaystyle 2\mathrm{d}_{0}\cdot(\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon})+\sqrt{\varepsilon}|\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}=0

holds for all
t\geq 0
.

Remark 2.1
.

For the remainder system (
1.33
)-(
1.35
) corresponding to the well-prepared initial data, the initial layer
\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\equiv 0
. If the initial constraint
2(\mathrm{d}_{0}\cdot\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon})(0,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}|\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}(0,x)=0
holds, then the constraint (
1.34
), hence
2\mathrm{d}_{0}\cdot\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}|\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}=0
still holds for all
t\geq 0
.

Before proving Lemma
2.2
, for convenience to readers, we list Lemma 4.1 in
[
8
]
as follows:

Lemma 2.3

(Lemma 4.1 in
[
8
]
)
.

Assume
(\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})
is a sufficiently smooth solution to the system (
1.1
)-(
1.8
). If the constraint
|\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|=1
is further assumed, then the Lagrangian multiplier
\bm{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}
is (
1.2
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}=-\varepsilon|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|\nabla\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\lambda_{2}\mathrm{A}^{\varepsilon}:\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\otimes\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\,.

Conversely, if we give the form of
\bm{\gamma}^{\varepsilon}
as (
1.2
) and
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}
satisfies the initial compatibility
|\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|\big|_{t=0}=1
and
(\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})|_{t=0}=0
, then
|\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|=1
holds at any time.

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

According to the formal analysis given in Section Introduction, we know that

(2.16)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{u}_{0}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\mathrm{d}_{0}(t,x)+\varepsilon\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\end{array}\right.

subject to the equations of (
1.1
) but without the geometric constraint
|\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|=1
. Via the initial relations (
1.24
), one easily derives from (
2.14
) and the compatibilities (
1.9
) that

(2.17)

\displaystyle|\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|(0,x)=1\,,\ (\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon})(0,x)=0\,.

Therefore, Lemma
2.3
implies that
|\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}|=1
holds for all
t\geq 0
. Noticing that
|\mathrm{d}_{0}(t,x)|\equiv 1
, we easily derive from the expression of
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
and the geometric constraint
\mathrm{d}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
that the constraint (
1.21
) holds for all
t\geq 0
. Then the proof of Lemma
2.2
is finished.
∎

The same arguments in justifying Lemma
2.2
will also imply the conclusions of Remark
2.1
, just letting
\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\equiv 0
.

3.
A priori uniform energy estimates for the remainder system

In this section, we will derive the a priori uniform (in small
\varepsilon>0
) energy estimates for the remainder systems (
1.20
) or (
1.33
) globally in times. Notice that there two differences between the equations (
1.20
) and (
1.33
):

1) the system (
1.20
) involves the terms
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon})
,
\varepsilon\mathrm{div}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{u}}(\mathrm{D}_{I})
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{d}}(\mathrm{D}_{I})
;

2) The initial conditions of (
1.20
) are nontrivial (see (
1.27
)) but that of (
1.33
) are all imposed on zero (see (
1.35
)).

However, the initial data (
1.27
) of (
1.20
) are infinitely small quantities and the initial layer
\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
defined in (
1.18
) is also infinitely small as
\varepsilon\ll 1
. The terms
\varepsilon\mathrm{div}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{u}}(\mathrm{D}_{I})
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{d}}(\mathrm{D}_{I})
involved in (
1.20
)-(
1.27
) will not result to any more difficulty in the energy estimates comparing to the energy estimates of the system (
1.33
)-(
1.35
). For term
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{D}_{I}^{\varepsilon})
, it will be dealt with the same way as the term
\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0})
, the details of which will be given later. To avoid the tedious calculations in controlling the tedious terms
\varepsilon\mathrm{div}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{u}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{d}}
, we only derive the a priori estimates of the remainder system (
1.33
) with the initial conditions (
1.35
), for simplicity. Actually, its calculations remain very annoying and complicated.

Next, we aim at deriving the a priori estimates of the remainder equations (
1.33
) with the initial data (
1.35
). The
key points
are:

•

The
H^{N}
-norm of term
\partial_{t}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0})
in the
\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
-equation of (
1.33
) has the same regularity as the
H^{N}
-norm of
\Delta\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
and the energy dissipative rate will only supply a regularity of
\|\nabla\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}
, so that we can not crudely view it as a source term to be controlled in the right-hand side of the energy inequality. To overcome this, we deal with this term as an energy term, just like
\partial_{t}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
or
\partial_{t}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
. Consequently, the energy functional of the remainder system (
1.33
) is depended on the vector field
\mathrm{d}_{0}
, which is a solution to the limit equations (
1.10
).

•

The relations (
2.1
) is essential to deal the the terms
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{u}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{d}}
, which are all linearly dependent on the
\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}
and with the coefficient
\mathrm{d}_{0}
. We derive some useful dissipative structures from these two terms. So, we will derive an energy dissipative rate of the remainder system depending also on
\mathrm{d}_{0}
.

Before deriving the a priori uniform energy estimates on the remainder system (
1.33
), we state the global existence, which has been proved by Wang-Zhang-Zhang in
[
21
]
, to the incompressible parabolic Ericksen-Leslie’s liquid crystal model (
1.10
) with small initial data (
1.13
). For convenience to readers, we restate this result here. We first define the following energy functionals
\mathscr{E}_{s,0}
and
\mathscr{D}_{s,0}
for any integer
s\geq 2
(see Section 5 of
[
21
]
):

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{s,0}(t)=

\displaystyle\|\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla\Delta^{s}\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\mathrm{u}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\Delta^{s}\mathrm{u}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{s,0}(t)=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{-\lambda_{1}}\|\Delta\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\tfrac{1}{-\lambda_{1}}\|\Delta^{s+1}\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{4}\|\nabla\mathrm{u}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{4}\|\nabla\Delta^{s}\mathrm{u}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,.

By interpolation, one easily know that

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{s,0}(t)

\displaystyle\thicksim\|\mathrm{u}_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{2s}}+\|\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{2s}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{s,0}(t)

\displaystyle\thicksim\|\nabla\mathrm{u}_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{2s}}+\|\Delta\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{2s}}\,.

Then, the following result holds:

Proposition 3.1

(Wang-Zhang-Zhang in
[
21
]
)
.

Let
s\geq 2
be an integer. Assume that the Leslie coefficients satisfy (
1.7
) and (
1.38
), and the initial data
\nabla\mathrm{d}^{in}\in H^{2s}
,
\mathrm{u}^{in}\in H^{2s}
. Then if there exists a
\beta_{s,0}>0
such that

\|\nabla\mathrm{d}^{in}\|^{2}_{H^{2s}}+\|\mathrm{u}^{in}\|^{2}_{H^{2s}}\leq\beta_{s,0}\,,

the incompressible parabolic Ericksen-Leslie’s liquid crystal model (
1.10
) with initial conditions (
1.13
) admits a unique global classical solution

\mathrm{u}_{0},\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{2s})\,,\ \nabla\mathrm{u}_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+};H^{2s})

satisfying the uniform bound

(3.3)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{u}_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{2s}}+\|\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{2s}}\leq c_{0}^{-1}\mathscr{E}_{s,0}\leq c_{0}^{-1}C_{0}\beta_{s,0}

holds for all
t\geq 0
. Moreover, the following energy inequality holds:

(3.4)

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathscr{E}_{s,0}+\mathscr{D}_{s,0}\leq 0\,,\quad\forall t\geq 0\,.

We now introduce the following energy functional
\mathscr{E}_{N,\varepsilon}(t)

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{N,\varepsilon}(t)=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\|\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}+(1-\delta)\|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\|\nabla\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}

\displaystyle+(\tfrac{-\delta\lambda_{1}}{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{5}{4}\delta)\|\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}+\|\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}+\tfrac{\delta}{2}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}

\displaystyle+\delta\|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}+2\sum_{|m|\leq N}\left\langle\partial^{m}(\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0}),\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle

and the energy dissipative rate
\mathscr{D}_{N,\varepsilon}(t)

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{N,\varepsilon}(t)=

\displaystyle\tfrac{3\mu_{4}}{8\varepsilon}\|\nabla\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}+\tfrac{\delta}{2\varepsilon}\|\nabla\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}-\delta\|\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{N}}

\displaystyle+\tfrac{\mu_{1}}{\varepsilon}\sum_{|m|\leq N}\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon}):\mathrm{d}_{0}\otimes\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(\mu_{5}+\mu_{6}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{\lambda_{1}})\sum_{|m|\leq N}\|(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\tfrac{-\lambda_{1}}{\varepsilon}\sum_{|m|\leq N}\|\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}+(\partial^{m}\mathrm{B}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}+\tfrac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}(\partial^{m}\mathrm{A}_{R}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\,,

where
\delta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
is a fixed constant, depending only on
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
and
N
.

One notices that the energy
\mathscr{E}_{N,\varepsilon}(t)
and the energy dissipative rate
\mathscr{D}_{N,\varepsilon}(t)
may not be nonnegative, since there is an indefinitely signed term
\sum_{|m|\leq N}\left\langle\partial^{m}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathrm{d}_{0},\partial^{m}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{u}_{0}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{u}_{R}^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}_{R}^{\varepsilon}\right\rangle
